# Brief config

Edit this file to retune the brief. The skill reads it every run. Nothing here is
permanent — the point is that it's cheap to change.

## Beats

Candidate pool, not a daily quota. Stories compete on merit; a beat with nothing
worth saying simply doesn't appear.

**Weighting.** The beats marked **Core** are why this brief exists. They lead the
issue and get the benefit of the doubt in a close call. Everything else is secondary
and has to earn its slot against them — see *Altitude bar*.

| Beat | Scope |
|---|---|
| **Core** — AI & tech industry | Model releases, agent tooling, capex and the physical constraints on it, the business and policy fights around them |
| **Core** — Macro & markets | Fed path, rates, inflation, employment, market-moving earnings |
| World economy | US, EU, China, Middle East — **only through the lens of US policy toward and interaction with each** |
| **Core** — US debt & fiscal | Deficit, Treasury issuance and buybacks, debt service, CBO projections, the long end of the curve |
| **Core** — US financials | Top US banks, asset managers, payments. JPMorgan and its direct competitors get standing attention |
| **Core** — US politics & policy | Legislation, courts, regulation. Substance only |
| Geopolitics | Conflicts and rivalries with real second-order consequences **for the US**. A foreign conflict with no US channel is not this brief's business |
| Science & public health | Research that changes how something works |
| **Core** — UAP disclosure | Government disclosure, implementing guidance, credible releases, new imagery from state or public sources. Track the paperwork, not just the imagery |
| Energy & grid | Generation, transmission, interconnection, transformers, retail power prices — where AI capex becomes a household bill |
| *Wildcard* | **Unset — tell the skill what this is** |

## Altitude bar

1. **Foreign stories need a US channel.** Ask what changes for this reader — a price
   they pay, a rate they borrow at, a policy their government must answer, a company
   they own. If the answer is "it is interesting", it does not run.
2. **Disruption is not consequence.** Cancelled flights, a factory's headcount, a
   single arrest — events, not developments, unless something structural moves
   behind them.

(A quiet Core beat still runs on a dated catalyst — that rule lives in the skill,
§3. This file's job is to say which beats are Core.)

## Local section

The skill renders this as its own section, judged on a local bar rather than a
national one. Leave the name blank to omit the section entirely.

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Section name | Florida & Tampa Bay |
| Scope | New Port Richey, Pasco, Pinellas, Tampa, state-level FL |

## Anti-topics

*Defaults. Edit freely.*

- Sports results and transfers
- Celebrity and entertainment industry news
- Crime blotter without policy consequence
- Horse-race polling and campaign tactics
- Personnel churn with no policy effect
- Product launches that are only marketing
- "New study finds" pieces with no accessible study
- Foreign corporate restructuring with no US channel
- Natural-disaster disruption — flights, closures, transit — with no US effect
- Individual acts of political violence with no policy consequence
- Foreign elections, unless the result changes a US relationship

## Settled preferences

*From reader feedback. Do not re-ask these in the per-story questions.*

- **Corrections** go in a box at the top of the affected story, not the footer.
- **When an earlier framing turns out wrong**, say so prominently — it earns space.
- **Single-sourced stories** are wanted, run with the `Single-sourced` label rather than held.
- **Trade stories**: both the legal-mechanism half and the market-effect half.
- **Ukraine**: current balance is right for a US-centred brief — do not increase or cut.
- **Florida section**: no target size. Only what clears a real local bar; empty is fine.
- **Oil strikes and the pump price** run as one causal chain, not two stories. (Asked again 11 Sep,
  because the Bab el-Mandeb is a different strait — answer pending.)
- **Charts**: wanted. Prefer the official series with a record line, not decoration.
- **Fed**: actual prints and the decision only. No state-of-play item on a quiet day.
- **Underreported finds**: current balance is right. Do not increase at the cost of a headline story.
- **Lab safety disclosures** and **state AI legislation**: both confirmed as standing threads.

**Standing discipline note, added 13 Sep after an error.** Never carry a weekday label
("Monday", "this Friday") forward between issues. Re-derive the day of the week from the
date every run. The 11 and 12 Sep issues both called 15 September "Monday"; it is a
Tuesday, and the wrong label was attached to two separate threads (duration of status,
Canada duty-list modifications). Dates were right throughout; only the weekday was wrong.
Corrected in the 13 Sep second edition.

**Standing discipline note, added 18 Sep after a worse error.** A market LEVEL printed to the basis point
must come from the issuer's own published series, not from a vendor quote and never from a search-engine
summary of one. On 14–17 September this brief printed the 10-year Treasury yield as 5.00, 5.041, 5.016 and
4.943 and built a lead around the 10-year going "through 5%" for the first time since July 2007. Treasury's
own Daily Par Yield Curve — reached on the first attempt on 18 September — shows 4.88, 4.91, 4.94 and 4.86.
**The 5% crossing did not happen in the series of record.** The sourcing caveat was disclosed each day; the
number was still wrong, and it was the number the story turned on. Corrected as the second lead on 18 September.
The generalisable rule: **when a vendor page is blocked, check whether the issuer publishes the number before
falling back to a summary of the blocked page.** See the Treasury entry under *Sources*.

**Standing discipline note, added 22 Sep after a wrong turn call.** **Do not call a trend turn off a daily series when a weekly official series exists for the same quantity.** The 21 Sep issue called "the top of a move" in diesel off three shrinking daily AAA increases (5.2c, 1.8c, 0.6c); on 22 Sep AAA rose 1.69c and undid it. The deceleration was real — it is visible in the EIA weekly increments (+36.8c, +31.8c, +24.4c) — and it was read off the wrong instrument. The generalisable form: **a daily card average measures today; a weekly survey measures the trend; a weekly average cannot re-accelerate on a Tuesday and a daily average cannot decelerate over three weeks.** Use the daily series for the level and the fastest honest signal, the weekly official series for direction. This is a cousin of the 18 Sep rule about issuer series versus vendor quotes, and of the standing caution about never pooling daily and weekly transit counts.

## Sources

**Reliably reachable:** Reuters · AP · BBC · Guardian · NPR · Al Jazeera · CNBC ·
ProPublica · SEC EDGAR · Federal Register · BLS · Federal Reserve · court dockets ·
company IR pages · trade press

**Non-English, reachable:** Le Monde · El País · Folha de S.Paulo · Nikkei Asia ·
SCMP · Haaretz · Yle · Handelsblatt and FAZ (partial)

**Paywalled, headline-only:** WSJ · FT · Bloomberg · NYT · The Economist. Treat as
signal that a story exists; stand it up elsewhere before using it.

**Local:** Tampa Bay Times · Suncoast News · WFLA · WUSF · Pasco and Pinellas county
agendas and minutes

**Not reachable:** X/Twitter (login wall). YouTube gives titles, descriptions, and
sometimes transcripts — never assume a video was watched. Reuters, AP, BBC, Guardian,
Le Monde and El País all refuse direct front-page fetches; reach their stories through
search instead. CNN returns 451. GAO's reports index returned 403 on 11 Sep. Tampa Bay
Times is paywalled beyond the lede.

**Operational notes that save a run:**
- **federalregister.gov blocks page fetches** and redirects to an unblock page. Use the
  JSON API instead — it works and is better: `https://www.federalregister.gov/api/v1/documents.json?conditions[publication_date][is]=YYYY-MM-DD&conditions[type][]=RULE&conditions[type][]=PRORULE&per_page=60&fields[]=title&fields[]=agencies&fields[]=type`.
  Add `&conditions[term]=…` to pull an abstract, docket, comment-close and effective date
  for one document. Caveat: this gives abstracts, not preambles — say so when it matters.
- **EIA's PDF releases cannot be parsed.** Use the weekly data tables at
  `eia.gov/dnav/pet/pet_pri_gnd_dcus_nus_w.htm` — same series, readable.
- **Handelsblatt's front page does fetch**, and is the most useful non-English source for
  European central banking and the global bond market. Confirmed again 12 Sep.
- **apnews.com refuses direct fetches** (hub and article pages alike, 12 Sep). Reach AP through
  search or through a syndicating outlet.
- **Al Jazeera's `/news/` index and its live blogs do fetch**, and are the fastest way to get a
  same-morning headline sweep when AP and Reuters front pages are unavailable.
- **BLS release pages (`bls.gov/news.release/cpi.nr0.htm`) fetch cleanly** and give the full
  component tables. Use the primary release, never a wire summary, for CPI and PPI numbers.
- **`text.npr.org` is the best general wire sweep available here (13 Sep).** It fetches cleanly,
  lists ~20 current headlines with no markup, and produced two lead stories on a Sunday when AP
  was refusing and `npr.org/sections/national/` returned 503. Caveat: an NPR story syndicated
  across dozens of member-station sites is **one** source, not dozens — the search results look
  like corroboration and are not.
- **`nhc.noaa.gov/text/MIATWOAT.shtml`** fetches cleanly and gives the full Atlantic outlook.
  Worth a check every run through November for the Florida section.
- **japantimes.co.jp returns HTTP 402** (13 Sep). Treat as paywalled.
- **OFAC notices in the Federal Register are frequently codification of licences issued months
  earlier.** Read the issue date inside the notice, not the publication date on it — four
  Venezuela general licences on 11 Sep looked like news and were not.
- **The Federal Register public-inspection list is offline from midnight Eastern** and returns
  Monday at 8:45am. On a Sunday run there is no view of Monday's documents at all — say so
  rather than implying the list was empty. The documents JSON API still works and is the way to
  pin an effective date (`conditions[term]=…&fields[]=effective_on`).
- **`text.npr.org` article links redirect to `www.npr.org` (14 Sep)** and the redirect is not followed automatically. The index page itself still fetches cleanly and remains the best general wire sweep here; to read a story, take the `nx-s1-…` slug and fetch `https://www.npr.org/<slug>` directly.
- **CNBC article pages return 403 consistently (14 Sep, three attempts).** Headlines and search summaries do come through, which is enough to establish that a story exists and roughly what it says — but stand the figures up elsewhere. NBC News fetches cleanly and covered the same market moves.
- **`congress.gov` returns 403 (14 Sep).** Bill status and latest actions cannot be verified directly. Do not claim a floor action without another source; POWER Magazine and trade press carried committee votes with dates.
- **Federal Register: presidential documents are `conditions[type][]=PRESDOCU`, not RULE or PRORULE.** A RULE/PRORULE query silently misses proclamations and executive orders entirely — the five §338 Canada proclamations were nearly missed on 14 Sep for exactly this reason. Add `&fields[]=proclamation_number&fields[]=signing_date&fields[]=citation` to tell a newly signed document from one that is merely being published. **And to read the actual text, the `raw_text_url` endpoint works** — `https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/full_text/text/YYYY/MM/DD/DOC-NUM.txt` — which is how the effective-date and severability language was quoted. That endpoint is the way around the unblock-page redirect on document pages.
- **`conditions[type][]=NOTICE` for the whole day is worth one call every run.** On 14 Sep it produced the USTR National Trade Estimate comment request and a FERC reliability docket that no thread pointed at. Note the FR abstracts frequently omit comment-close dates that are in the document itself.
- **`flrules.org/gateway/ruleno.asp?id=<rule>` fetches cleanly** and gives a full dated notice history for a Florida rule, which is the primary source for whether a hearing has actually been noticed. Far better than searching for news about it.
- **`pinellas.gov/budget/` fetches; `pascocountyfl.net/171/Budget` now 301s to `pascocountyfl.gov/171/Budget`, which returned 404 on 14 Sep.**
- **Insurance Journal refused the connection outright (14 Sep); safety4sea returns 403.** So marine war-risk premiums remain hard to source here, and the Joint War Committee listing decision is still unverified. Al Jazeera's economy desk is the only reachable source carrying premium figures, and it publishes them without dates or market attribution — usable as an indication, not as a print.
- **Wikipedia's current-events portal is earning its place as the negative check.** On 13 Sep it caught the Congo Ebola epidemic; on 14 Sep it had the Hanish islands seizure and the Dangote listing before either appeared on a reachable wire front page. It is a tertiary source and nothing should be published on it alone — but as a *list of what happened that you have not seen*, nothing else here comes close.
- **`darioamodei.com`'s index page lags its own posts (13 Sep).** The 12 Sep essay was not listed
  there, but `darioamodei.com/post/we-must-pace-the-frontier` resolved. Try the slugged `/post/`
  URL before concluding a primary document is unreachable — the morning edition ran that story
  single-sourced for exactly this reason.
- **When several outlets block the same story at once, go for the primary document.** Axios,
  CNBC, Deadline and the Detroit News all returned 403 or 402 on the Amodei essay on 13 Sep;
  Deadline redirects to a `tollbit.` host that also 403s. The essay itself fetched.
- **`npr.org/sections/news/` timed out and `bbc.com` refused (13 Sep)** — use `text.npr.org` and
  Al Jazeera's index instead. **`wfla.com` returned 403; `fox13news.com` tag feeds** (e.g.
  `/tag/us/fl/pasco`) fetch cleanly and are the better Tampa Bay local sweep.
- **The FR documents API's NOTICE sweep is now proven twice over and should be treated as a standing daily call, not an optional one.** On 14 Sep it produced the USTR National Trade Estimate request; on 15 Sep it produced the entire SNAP food-restriction story — eighteen notices, one policy, visible only by reading the whole day's output rather than the entry you came for. Pair it with `conditions[type][]=RULE&conditions[type][]=PRORULE` and `conditions[type][]=PRESDOCU`; three calls cover the day.
- **`federalregister.gov/documents/full_text/text/YYYY/MM/DD/DOC-NUM.txt` is the workhorse** and got round the unblock-page redirect again on 15 Sep for the SNAP notices and the third-party risk guidance. Use it whenever the abstract is thin — the abstracts omitted the comment deadlines and the implementation dates that made the SNAP story.
- **`eia.gov/dnav/pet/hist/LeafHandler.ashx?n=pet&s=emd_epd2d_pte_nus_dpg&f=w` gives the full weekly diesel history**, which the summary table page does not — but it **mislabels month boundaries**, and on 15 Sep returned the correct seven values under dates shifted four weeks early. Always triangulate the dates against figures already in this file before charting. The 2022 record ($5.810, w/e 20 Jun 2022) came through correctly.
- **`prohumanassembly.org` and `futureoflife.org` both fetch**, and are better than the wire write-ups for what an advocacy event actually is — programme, times, speaker list, and the declaration text.
- **`text.npr.org`'s index fetched cleanly on 15 Sep but every direct article fetch timed out at 60s** (three attempts, both `npr.org/<slug>` and the dated URL form). Reaching the story through search, or through a syndicating member station such as WJCT or Ideastream, worked every time. Budget for this: the index is a headline list, not a way to read anything.
- **`clerk.house.gov/Votes/<year><rollcall>` fetches cleanly and is authoritative (16 Sep).** It gives bill number, the exact question, date, time to the minute, and the full party breakdown — roll call 307 produced the whole war-powers lead. Pair it with **`senate.gov/legislative/LIS/roll_call_lists/vote_menu_119_2.htm`**, which lists recent Senate votes with question, result and tally and is the way to find a vote number you do not already have. Do **not** guess a vote number and fetch the detail page directly: a guess at 00220 on 16 Sep returned an August CDC nomination vote, and the wrong vote looks exactly as authoritative as the right one.
- **Member and committee press-release pages fetch cleanly** (`gallego.senate.gov`, `moulton.house.gov`, 16 Sep) and are primary for a member's stated reasoning, which wire copy compresses out. Use them for *why* a vote went as it did.
- **congress.gov still 403s (16 Sep, third day).** Bill *text* remains unreadable here. The Clerk's question line plus a sponsor's release is the workaround, but it means the operative clauses of a resolution cannot be checked — say so.
- **The Federal Register NOTICE sweep produced the underreported lead for a third consecutive run (16 Sep):** six Commerce AD/CVD final determinations on solar cells, scattered among eighty notices dominated by NAGPRA repatriations and SEC fee filings. **The tell is repetition — the same product name appearing four, six, eighteen times in one day's list is a policy, not a filing.** Treat it as a standing daily call alongside RULE/PRORULE and PRESDOCU.
- **`federalregister.gov/documents/full_text/text/…` again did all the real work (16 Sep).** The abstracts for the solar determinations omit every rate; the CDC Ebola order's abstract omits the case counts, the contact-tracing figure and the LPR coverage that made the story. **Never write from a Federal Register abstract when the full text endpoint exists.**
- **`tradingeconomics.com/commodity/brent-crude-oil` fetches and gives a level plus day/month/year changes** — but on 16 Sep it read $107.61 while Handelsblatt's front page read $102.65 the same morning and a third quote had $109.21 for 15 Sep. **A ~$6.50 spread across three feeds. "Brent" is variously front-month, next-month, dated physical and CFDs, and in backwardation the adjacent months are far apart.** Quote one feed, name it, and prefer physical indicators (pipeline restart, chokepoint transits, AAA at the pump) over the screen price. ICE settlements have not been reachable here.
- **`freightwaves.com` fetches cleanly and is the best off-beat source found so far for the diesel pass-through.** Its front page carries the Cass monthly indexes, carrier earnings and bankruptcies. **Critical distinction to preserve: Cass's truckload LINEHAUL index excludes fuel surcharges.** A rise in it is capacity and demand, never diesel — diesel passes through separately on the surcharge line, indexed to the EIA weekly print. Conflating them will produce a wrong pass-through story.
- **`scmp.com/news/china` fetches cleanly (16 Sep)** and carried the Bessent–He Lifeng weekend meeting and the 24 Sep Xi state-visit date before any reachable US wire front page. Good standing check for the China leg of any thread.
- **`pascocountyfl.gov`'s budget-adoption page served two different schedules to two fetches the same morning (16 Sep)** — 3 Sep / 16 Sep and 9 Sep / 22 Sep. One of them is last year's FY2026 calendar out of cache. **Read a county page twice before relying on a date, and cross-check against this file's own earlier entries.** `pasco.legistar.com/Calendar.aspx` returns "Invalid parameters!" and is not a fallback.
- **`philadelphiafed.org/surveys-and-data/regional-economic-analysis/mbos-YYYY-MM` fetches cleanly (17 Sep)** and gives every diffusion index plus the share-of-firms detail the wires drop. Worth a standing monthly check around the 15th–20th: on 17 Sep it produced **prices received +14 points to 31.3** — the pass-through signal — that no reachable wire carried. **But know the instrument: it is a DIFFUSION index for the Third District (eastern PA, southern NJ, Delaware), counting the SHARE of firms reporting an increase net of decreases. It does not measure magnitude, and it can rise sharply on many small increases. Never convert it into an inflation forecast.**
- **`everycrsreport.com` fetches cleanly and is the best statutory-mechanics source reachable here (17 Sep)** — LSB11474 gave the entire section-by-section structure of the Graham Act and seven named ambiguities, which is precisely what congress.gov's 403 has been denying since 14 Sep. **Treat everycrsreport as the standing workaround for bill mechanics.** Committee press pages (`waysandmeans.house.gov`) also fetch and are primary for a chairman's framing.
- **`dol.gov/ui/data.htm` returned 503 and `dol.gov/ui/data.pdf` is binary that cannot be parsed (17 Sep).** Weekly claims had to come from Bloomberg/FXStreet reporting. Likewise **`census.gov/construction/nrc/current/` renders its release as IMAGES** — the figures are not extractable and must come from reporting. Budget for both: two of the most routine federal releases are not directly readable here.
- **`clerk.house.gov/Votes` returns no vote data in its HTML (17 Sep)** — only the search interface. The detail pages at `clerk.house.gov/Votes/<year><rollcall>` do work (proven 16 Sep) but you must already have the number, and guessing is how you get an August CDC nomination vote instead. If you cannot find the number, the tally has to come from reporting and the party breakdown should not be claimed.
- **CNBC article pages have now 403'd six consecutive days (12–17 Sep).** Headlines and search-result summaries still come through, which is enough to establish a figure exists and roughly what it is — but a number sourced that way is second-hand and should be labelled Single-sourced, as the 10yr 4.943% was on 17 Sep.
- **`openai.com` returns 403 (17 Sep, two attempts, both the index and a specific `/index/<slug>/` post) and `apolloresearch.ai` blog slugs 404.** So OpenAI primary documents are effectively unreachable here, unlike Anthropic's and darioamodei.com's. **MarkTechPost carried much the fullest account of the 17 Sep misalignment framework — all three review tracks and all six incidents with rates — and is worth trying early for AI-lab publications.**
- **Wikipedia's current-events portal was UNPOPULATED for 17 Sep when read mid-morning.** It has been the best negative-check instrument available (it caught the Congo Ebola epidemic and the Hanish seizure) but it lags on the day itself. **On a same-day run, expect to run the negative check by hand against Al Jazeera's index and Handelsblatt with the beat list held alongside — and say in the provenance line that the usual instrument was unavailable.**
- **`npr.org` article pages timed out on every direct attempt (17 Sep, four tries), including the dated `/YYYY/MM/DD/<slug>/<title>` form.** Member stations work reliably — `nprillinois.org`, `wxpr.org`, `stlpr.org`, `wunc.org`, `kpbs.org` — and are the way to actually read an NPR story. Remember they are ONE source, not many.
- **DATE TRAP, hit and avoided 17 Sep: searching for OpenAI safety research surfaces the SEPTEMBER 2025 "detecting and reducing scheming" paper (o3 13%→0.4%, o4-mini 8.7%→0.3%, deliberative alignment, ~30x) above the 2026 item.** Two OpenAI safety publications a year apart, both mid-September, both about deceptive model behaviour. **Check the year on every result before using a figure from it.**
- **TREASURY'S DAILY PAR YIELD CURVE FETCHES CLEANLY AND IS THE SERIES OF RECORD (18 Sep, first attempt).** `home.treasury.gov/resource-center/data-chart-center/interest-rates/TextView?type=daily_treasury_yield_curve&field_tdr_date_value_month=YYYYMM` returns a dated table with 2/10/20/30-year rates. **Use it for every dated yield LEVEL. It contradicted four consecutive days of 10-year figures this file had printed from search-engine summaries of CNBC's quote page — see the correction in [Treasury buybacks & debt service]. A ticker or a newspaper front page may be used for DIRECTION ONLY. A yield from a search-result summary is unusable to the basis point.** The lesson generalises past yields: when a paywalled or blocked vendor page is the only apparent route to a number, check whether the ISSUER publishes it.
- **PDFs ARE THE LARGEST STANDING SOURCING WEAKNESS AND ON 18 SEP THEY COST THREE STORIES THEIR PRIMARY SOURCE.** `boj.or.jp/en/mopo/mpmdeci/mpr_2026/k260918a.pdf` (BoJ statement), `berkshirehathaway.com/news/sep1826.pdf` (Berkshire release) and `floridahealth.gov/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/fl-arbovirus-report-w36-2026.pdf` (DOH week 36 dengue) all FETCHED and all returned unparseable binary, so three stories resting on primary documents were written from secondary accounts and labelled Single-sourced. **Budget for this: if a story's load-bearing document is a PDF, assume it cannot be read and find who has read it — a member station, a trade outlet, an issuer's HTML summary. Say in the issue that the document was not read.** Two figures lost this way on 18 Sep that mattered: the BoJ vote tally, and the PCR serotyping line in the dengue report.
- **Florida DOH arbovirus report URL pattern, confirmed 18 Sep:** `floridahealth.gov/wp-content/uploads/2026/MM/fl-arbovirus-report-wNN-2026.pdf`. The index at `floridahealth.gov/statistics-data/population-surveillance/arbovirus-surveillance/` fetches cleanly and is the fastest way to establish WHETHER a week has published — which is what nine issues of recorded absence turned on. The report PDF itself will not parse; WUSF's health desk reads it reliably within a day.
- **`gcaptain.com` fetches cleanly and is the best maritime source found so far (18 Sep).** Its front page carried Hormuz and Bab el-Mandeb DAILY TRANSIT COUNTS from Reuters preliminary shipping data — the measurement both chokepoint threads have wanted for a week — plus the Chinese approach to Tehran over the Houthis and an IMO warning, none of which reached a general wire front page. **It does NOT carry war-risk premiums or JWC listings, so it is not a substitute for Insurance Journal or safety4sea, both still unreachable.** Remember the transit figures are DAILY and must never be pooled with Lloyd's List Intelligence's weekly averages.
- **`ofac.treasury.gov/recent-actions` fetches, and so does the dated detail page at `ofac.treasury.gov/recent-actions/YYYYMMDD`** (18 Sep), which gives general licence numbers, titles, FAQ numbers and delisted entities in full. Better and faster than waiting for the Federal Register codification — and it avoids the trap recorded 11 Sep of mistaking codification for news.
- **`dtnpf.com/agriculture/web/ag/news` fetches cleanly and is the best off-beat source for the diesel pass-through into agriculture (18 Sep).** It carried the Saudi East–West restart plan and its effect on crude two days before a general wire did, plus farmer-level reporting on record diesel costs ahead of harvest. **Worth a standing check while the fuel shock runs.**
- **`energy.gov/ceser/northeast-home-heating-oil-reserve` fetches** and gives the site-by-site barrel breakdown. Use it whenever "strategic reserves" come up: the SPR is crude, and the only federal DISTILLATE reserve is one million barrels of ULSD sized for a New England winter.
- **`folha.uol.com.br` and `elpais.com` both refused the connection outright (18 Sep)** — Claude Code could not fetch either host. So the reachable non-English set is now effectively European only (Handelsblatt, plus Nikkei Asia and SCMP in English). This is why the outside-the-watchlist source share fell just short of half on 18 Sep. **Find a reachable Latin American and a reachable South Asian source.**
- **`gao.gov/reports-testimonies` returned 403 again (18 Sep, second time this month).** Treat GAO as unreachable.
- **NPR article pages have now timed out four consecutive days (15–18 Sep).** Member stations remain the reliable route — `stlpr.org/npr/YYYY-MM-DD/<slug>` and `wusf.org` both worked on 18 Sep. **Two stories were lost to this on 18 Sep and are carried forward: a DHS email on the administration's election plans, and lawsuits to enforce the bar on armed federal officers at polling places. Both are squarely on the US politics beat.**
- **`whitehouse.gov/presidential-actions/` FETCHES CLEANLY AND IS THE FASTEST ROUTE TO A SIGNED PRESIDENTIAL INSTRUMENT (20 Sep) — it produced the lead.** The index lists the ten most recent actions with dates, and individual `/presidential-actions/YYYY/MM/<slug>/` pages give full operative text. **This beats the Federal Register API by DAYS: the 18 Sep H-1B proclamation and executive order had not published in the FR when checked 20 Sep, and a PRESDOCU query for 18–22 Sep returned exactly one unrelated document.** Companion `/fact-sheets/YYYY/MM/<slug>/` pages also fetch and carry the administration's own figures. **Make this a standing daily call alongside the three FR API queries — the FR is the record, this is the wire.** Caveat: the index is only ten items deep, so an action older than ~48 hours may have scrolled off; the bill signing of 18 Sep was already gone. Slugs can carry typos (the H-1B EO's URL repeats the word "integrity") — take the slug from a search result rather than constructing it.
- **`statnews.com` fetches cleanly and is the best specialist health/biotech source found so far (20 Sep).** Its front page produced the Medicare WISeR AI prior-authorisation lead, which was on no thread, in no beat line and in no prior issue. **Worth a standing rotation slot as the trade publication for a sector not on the beat list.** `eff.org/deeplinks` also fetches and is where FOIA-obtained federal documents surface first.
- **"BRENT" IS NOW TWO DIFFERENT PRICES AND THE GAP IS $20–30 (20 Sep).** Front-month ICE futures read $98.54 on Handelsblatt's ticker while **Dated Brent — the physical North Sea cargo assessment — was ~$122 on 15 Sep (~$14 over futures) and reported above $131 by 19 Sep.** Cause: the futures/physical arbitrage requires chartering a ship, and vessels are unobtainable, so the two instruments have decoupled. **NEVER quote a screen Brent figure as "the price of oil" while the freight market is dislocated — say which instrument.** Energy Intelligence carries the dated assessment; `tradingeconomics.com` and newspaper tickers carry futures. **Freight figures diverge just as badly: $200,000, $800,000 and $1.2m/day all appear for "the VLCC rate" in September 2026, because they are different routes, dates and rate conventions. Name the route or do not print the number.**
- **COLUMN-POSITION RULE, added 21 Sep after the second wrong ten-year in four days. When pulling a value out of a wide numeric table, ENUMERATE THE COLUMN HEADERS IN THE SAME REQUEST AND COUNT THE POSITION — never trust the label a summarising fetch attaches.** Treasury's par-yield page now has 23 columns including a **1.5-month** tenor inserted between 1 Mo and 2 Mo, and several bill columns that render as N/A. The 18 and 20 Sep reads published the **7-year as the "10-year"** and the **1-year as the "2-year"**, while the 20-year and 30-year — which can be counted backwards from the right-hand edge — were correct both times. **Counting backwards from the edge is the sanity check.** A second fetch of the same page asking explicitly for "the column headers in exact left-to-right order, then the complete row" resolved it on the first attempt. This generalises to any wide table: EIA's LeafHandler, BLS component tables, FR API rows.
- **MONDAY-RUN RULE, added 21 Sep after three core-beat misses in one weekend.** The source-led gather works for documents published TODAY and FAILED for stories published on Friday, because a front page has moved on by Monday. **The Accenture evaluator deal (18 Sep), the AI antitrust suit (18 Sep) and the SEC proxy rescission (16 Sep) were each 3–5 days old before this brief reached them, all three on core beats.** A Monday run needs an EXPLICIT SWEEP OF FRIDAY AND THE WEEKEND on each core beat — a dated search per beat — not just a sweep of today.
- **AAA's state-extremes widget is ambiguous about which fuel it ranks.** On 13 Sep it showed a
  highest-state figure inconsistent with the ~$7.98 California diesel number in this file. Use
  the national averages, which are unambiguous; confirm the fuel before quoting state extremes.

- **TREASURY'S CSV ENDPOINT IS THE ANSWER TO THE COLUMN-POSITION PROBLEM AND IT SHOULD NOW BE THE FIRST READ (22 Sep).** `home.treasury.gov/resource-center/data-chart-center/interest-rates/daily-treasury-rates.csv/2026/all?type=daily_treasury_yield_curve&field_tdr_date_value=2026&page&_format=csv` returns an unambiguous header row — `Date,"1 Mo","1.5 Month","2 Mo","3 Mo","4 Mo","6 Mo","1 Yr","2 Yr","3 Yr","5 Yr","7 Yr","10 Yr","20 Yr","30 Yr"`, **FOURTEEN tenor columns** — followed by clean dated rows. **This explains the 18 and 20 Sep errors completely: the TextView page advertises 23 headers because it prepends eight BILL columns (6/8/17/52-week bank-discount and coupon-equivalent pairs) that render empty for recent dates, so the 14 returned values map to headers 10–23 and counting from the LEFT yields the 7-year as the "10-year". THE CSV HAS NO PHANTOM COLUMNS.** Read the CSV first, use TextView only to confirm. Both agreed on 22 Sep: 18 Sep 10yr 5.01, 21 Sep 10yr 4.96.
- **`un.org`'s Independent International Scientific Panel on AI PUBLISHES THEMATIC BRIEFS AND THEY FETCH (22 Sep) — it produced the lead.** `un.org/independent-international-scientific-panel-ai/en/thematic-briefs/<slug>` returns the full advance-unedited text. **This is now the most valuable AI-beat document source found, because it publishes facts about real incidents that neither the labs nor the wires carry, and it is a source no thread pointed at — it was reached by following an unrelated story's trail.** `uneca.org/stories/...` carries readable UN-system summaries with quotes when the primary brief is dense. Make it a standing rotation slot.
- **`grocerydive.com` fetches and is the right trade publication for the diesel-to-grocery pass-through (22 Sep).** Its front page carried the volume decline across meat, produce and bakery and the "primed to rise" inflation item. **Caveat: constructed article URLs 404 — take the slug from a search result, or reach the same reporting through `foodnavigator.com`, which also fetches.** Pair with `dtnpf.com` (farm-level) and `freightwaves.com` (carrier-level) for the three rungs of the pass-through.
- **`presidentti.fi/en/news/` returns 404 (22 Sep)**, so a declaration released through the Finnish presidency cannot be read at source here. That is why the 22 Sep AI-declaration story printed no signatory roll. Find a reachable route to Nordic head-of-state releases.
- **`supremecourt.gov/orders/ordersofthecourt/26` contains NO order list in its HTML (22 Sep)** — only navigation and explanatory text. It is not a usable check for whether an order issued. Needs a different route before the Court counts as a rotating source again.
- **EIA'S WEEKLY PETROLEUM STATUS REPORT IS A PDF AND WILL NOT PARSE (`ir.eia.gov/wpsr/wpsrsummary.pdf`, 22 Sep).** **This is a standing hole in the fuel-price thread: the two numbers that would signal relief — distillate inventories and refinery utilisation — are in a document this session cannot read, every Wednesday.** Find who reads it: FreightWaves, DTN and gCaptain all carry the distillate figure within a day. Budget for the lag.
- **THE FR NOTICE SWEEP'S REPETITION TELL FIRED AGAIN AND PRODUCED A LEAD (22 Sep): TEN DoD arms-sales notifications in one issue, and the repeated item inside them was APKWS-II air-to-air guidance sections — 8,211 across three documents.** The notifications are `NOTICE` type and the unclassified text is only in the full-text endpoint; the API abstract says nothing but "The DoD is publishing the unclassified text of an arms sales notification" and gives **no country, no equipment and no value**. **So the ten have to be fetched individually — ten calls — and there is no shortcut. Worth it: nothing in any wire or trade outlet carried any of them.** The tell generalises: a document TYPE appearing ten times in one day is a policy, not a filing, in the same way a product NAME appearing six times is.
- **AAA's front page gives regular petrol and diesel with yesterday/week/month/year comparators in one fetch and is reliable (twelve consecutive runs).** Keep straight that **AAA quotes REGULAR** while **EIA's weekly table quotes ALL GRADES** — on 22 Sep EIA all-grades petrol rose 15.5c on the week while AAA regular fell 0.36c on the day, and those are not in conflict. Enumerate EIA's column headers in the same request per the standing rule; on 22 Sep the table returned exactly three columns (Date, Gasoline All Grades, Diesel On-Highway) and the 14 Sep all-grades value cross-checked against this file's own earlier figure.

**Standing document sources worth rotating through (added 11 Sep):** Federal Register
daily issue · OFAC/Treasury sanctions press room · FERC newsroom and dockets · GAO
reports · FCC supply-chain pages · FDA device classifications · White House
proclamations and fact sheets · ECB monetary-policy decisions · Lloyd's List
Intelligence Red Sea brief and chokepoint transit data.

**Reader's trusted / distrusted list:** *unset — add names and the skill will weight
them, and say so when it leans on one.*

- **THE TREASURY COLUMN TRAP RECURRED ON 25 SEP AND WAS CAUGHT BY THE 21 SEP RULE.** A first read of the September page returned 4.93 / 4.89 / 4.89 / 5.05 / 5.10 for the 18th, 21st, 22nd, 23rd and 24th — **those are the 7-year column.** A second request asking for the headers numbered and every cell in position order showed a **26-column** table (it had 23 on 21 Sep), the 10-year at **column 24**, and the true values 5.01 / 4.96 / 4.96 / 5.11 / 5.18. **The position is not stable between runs, so the enumeration must happen every single time — a memorised column number is a wrong answer waiting.** Counting backwards from the right edge still protects the 20-year (25) and 30-year (26).
- **`freddiemac.com/pmms` fetches cleanly and is the primary source for the weekly mortgage survey (25 Sep).** It gives the 30- and 15-year averages with prior-week and year-ago figures. The gcs-web press-release host returned 503, so use the PMMS page itself. Worth a standing Friday check while the long end is moving.
- **`prh.noaa.gov/cphc` does not resolve from here (25 Sep, DNS failure)** — the Central Pacific Hurricane Center's own advisories are unreachable, and `nhc.noaa.gov/text/MIATCPCP1.shtml` 404s. For a Hawaii storm, `nhc.noaa.gov/text/MIATWOEP.shtml` (Eastern Pacific outlook) confirms existence, position and intensity; the warning areas and rainfall figures have to come from Hawaii News Now, weather.com or the Star-Advertiser, all downstream of the same NWS product.
- **NPR article pages returned 503 on every attempt (25 Sep), a change from the previous timeouts.** `text.npr.org`'s index still fetches and remains the best headline list here, but treat it as a list of what exists, never a way to read anything. Member stations and `nbcnews.com` both worked.
- **`clerk.house.gov/Votes/<year><rollcall>` and `senate.gov/legislative/LIS/roll_call_votes/vote1192/vote_119_2_00NNN.htm` both fetch and are authoritative (25 Sep).** For the Senate, `.../roll_call_lists/vote_menu_119_2.htm` gives recent vote numbers so you need not guess. **Two cautions learned on the Iran vote: a summariser may report a party subtotal that contradicts the names it lists, so check the subtotal against the chamber split (53 R / 47 D) before printing it; and remember a tie fails, because the Vice President breaks it — 49–50 was two votes short of passage, not one.**

- **Saturday is structurally the weakest source day and the run should be planned around it (26 Sep).** The `Federal Register` documents API returns a count of **0** — it does not publish on Saturdays, and the public-inspection list is offline until Monday 8:45am. The instrument that produced two of six leads on Friday produced nothing. Wikipedia's current-events portal was also nearly empty for the day and carried nothing at all for the previous day. Plan a Saturday run around courts, weather products and the wires, and say on the face of the issue that the document channel was closed.
- **`supremecourt.gov/orders/ordersofthecourt/26` serves a template with no order data (26 Sep).** Two emergency-docket orders issued the previous afternoon and neither appeared. So a same-weekend Supreme Court story cannot be sourced to the Court itself here: use a local public radio station in the affected state (`stlpr.org` had the docket number, 26A388, and the quoted language), the winning agency's own press release (`dhs.gov` gave the case name and the vote), and an election-administration outlet such as `votebeat.org` for what the order does in practice rather than what it holds.
- **NWS warning products are reachable through alert aggregators when weather.gov is not tried or fails.** `govonestop.com/nws/...` and `defconlevel.com/alert/...` both carry the full issuing office, issue time, expiry and hazard text — NWS Mount Holly's coastal flood warning and high wind warning came through this way on 26 September. Treat them as relays of a primary product, not as independent sources.
- **`prh.noaa.gov/cphc` failed DNS for a second consecutive day (26 Sep).** The Central Pacific Hurricane Center is effectively unreachable here. For a Hawaii storm: `nhc.noaa.gov/text/MIATWOEP.shtml` gives existence, position and hurricane status; everything else — intensity, warning areas, rainfall, declarations — has to come from NBC, CNN, Hawaii News Now or the Star-Advertiser, all downstream of the same NWS product and therefore **one** source, not four.
- **The Treasury table's column count changed AGAIN, from 26 on 25 Sep to 21 on 26 Sep, with a different ordering.** The 10-year was column 24 on Friday and column 13 on Saturday. The enumerate-and-count-by-position rule held both times, and Saturday's read cross-validated Friday's Thursday row exactly. **Never carry a column index between runs.**
- **When the subject of a story is Anthropic, disclose it in the story and again in the footer, and run it anyway (26 Sep).** The D.C. Circuit upheld the Pentagon's supply-chain-risk designation against Anthropic; this brief is written by Claude. The config's rule about never softening coverage of the reader's own interests applies with more force, not less, to the author's. Holding the story would itself be an editorial choice and a less visible one than covering it with a disclosure box.

- **`nhc.noaa.gov/mobile/text/refresh/HFOTCPCP2+html/<DDHHMM>.shtml` IS THE WAY TO READ A CENTRAL PACIFIC ADVISORY (27 Sep).** The CPHC's own host `prh.noaa.gov/cphc` has failed DNS three days running, but the NHC mobile text mirror carries the full CPHC public advisory — number, position, winds, pressure, movement, watches and warnings, rainfall and surf. The filename is the product's day-hour-minute in UTC. This turned a second-hand weather story into a primary-source one; use it for every Central Pacific storm.
- **A Sunday run is the weakest of the week and a weekend is worse in sequence.** The `Federal Register` returned zero documents on both 26 and 27 September, and Wikipedia's current-events portal had one entry for the 27th and none at all for the 26th. Two closed days in a row left five of seven leads and six of seven short items single-sourced or carrying an explicit note about what was not read. **Plan for it: on a weekend the primary-document channel is shut, so budget the run for courts, weather products, agency press pages and the daily AAA print, and say on the face of the issue that the document channel was closed rather than letting the confidence markers carry it alone.**
- **NPR article pages returned 503 for a third consecutive day (25-27 Sep), a change from the earlier timeouts.** `text.npr.org`'s index still fetches and is still the best headline list here. Member stations remain the way to read the stories and the rotation that worked this weekend was `opb.org`, `kpbs.org`, `stlpr.org`, `houstonpublicmedia.org`, `nprillinois.org`. **A dozen member stations running one NPR piece is ONE source; the search results look like corroboration and are not.**
- **When a lead rests on an announcement rather than a rule, say which and name the docket that will settle it.** The fuel economy rollback was announced on a Saturday with details promised for Monday and no `Federal Register` document in existence; the 34.5 mpg figure is the December 2025 proposal as reported. Mark it Single-sourced, state that the rule does not exist yet, and point at the NHTSA docket.
- **A storm can intensify while every coastal warning for it is cancelled, and both facts are correct.** Nolo strengthened to 100mph on 26 September while the tropical storm warning for Hawaii County and the watch for Maui County were both dropped, because the system was moving away as its core deepened. Sources split accordingly — one outlet led on 'downgraded to Category 1', another on 'may become Cat 3'. **Resolve it by reading the advisory rather than the headlines: the wind field leaving and the core deepening are independent.**

- **STANDING DISCIPLINE NOTE, added 28 Sep after the largest correction this brief has run. A MISSING DOCUMENT IS EVIDENCE ABOUT PROCESS, NEVER ABOUT SUBSTANCE.** On 25, 26 and 27 September this brief reported the absence of a Trump-Xi readout accurately and then drew an inference from it, ending with "the plainest reading is that there was nothing both sides would sign." On 28 September the two governments published a joint list for tariff cuts on $60bn of trade, coal purchase commitments and a standing quarterly Board of Trade. **The observation was right and the inference was wrong, for four consecutive issues.** The generalisable rule: when a document that would settle a question has not appeared, report its absence and stop. Do not convert "no text yet" into "no agreement", "no decision" or "nothing happened" — and when tempted, check a non-US front page first. Handelsblatt carried the tariff package before any reachable US wire did, for the second time this month.
- **`transportation.gov/briefing-room` fetches cleanly and is the primary source for a DOT announcement before the FR notice exists (28 Sep).** It gave the full CAFE proposal — the MY2022-2031 reset, the per-year percentages for cars and light trucks, the 34.5 mpg 2031 figure, the elimination of credit trading from MY2028, and the "developed without consideration of electric vehicles and credit trading" methodology line — none of which was in the weekend announcement the story ran on. **Check an agency's own briefing room the morning after a presidential announcement; it is usually a day ahead of the Federal Register.**
- **EIA's weekly retail price page publishes on a TUESDAY cycle, not Monday.** On 28 September the page's latest reading was still 21 September and it gave a next release date of **29 September**. The 27 September issue said the print was due "tomorrow" meaning the 28th, and had to correct it. **Read the "Next Release Date" off the page rather than assuming a Monday survey means a Monday publication.**
- **The whole-day Federal Register NOTICE and RULE sweep has now produced an underreported lead in four of the last six runs.** On 28 September it gave two leads (the FAA single-pilot rescission, the EPA gasoline distribution proposal) and two short items (the ITC tin mill schedule, the Cold Case Review Board determination) out of four full-text reads. **Treat the three API calls plus two or three `full_text/text/` reads as the non-negotiable core of a weekday run.** The tell for which entry matters is still repetition and the mismatch between a dull title and a live effective date.
- **Brent's three-feed spread is unchanged since 16 September and should be treated as permanent.** On 28 September: Handelsblatt $100.36, tradingeconomics $106.89, a third feed $107.34 — a seven-dollar spread on one morning. **Quote the direction, name both levels, and prefer physical indicators.** gCaptain's front page gave the two that mattered: Gulf of Oman ship-to-ship transfer capacity exhausted, and Qatari LNG traffic through Hormuz at a two-month high.

- **THE FR WHOLE-DAY SWEEP NOW HAS A PATTERN TO TRACK, NOT JUST STORIES (29 Sep).** Three rules took effect on publication with no notice and comment in a single morning, all citing the same APA exemption for rules on 'grants, benefits, or contracts'. **The tell is the pairing of a dull title with an immediate effective date**: "Recodification of Title IX Rules" and "Removing Constitutional Concerns From SBA Programs" both read as housekeeping and both changed enforcement. Read the `full_text/text/` for any RULE whose effective date is the publication date, whatever the title says. Opened as a thread; see [Immediate-effect rulemaking & the grants exemption].
- **`time.com` fetches cleanly and was the best source for a statutory mechanism (29 Sep).** Its pocket-rescission explainer gave the Impoundment Control Act's 45-day continuous-session clock, the GAO's exact reasoning, the 28 August 2025 precedent and the $810m component breakdown — none of which was in the wire copy. **For "how does this instrument work", try an explainer from a magazine before a wire.**
- **A component breakdown that does not sum to the headline is usually the honest state of the reporting, not an error.** The $810m rescission's published components total $617m; DHS migrant services and HUD housing counselling carried no figure in any account found. **Chart only what is quantified and say in the caption why the bars do not add up** — do not derive the remainder by subtraction and present it as a figure.
- **Member-station rotation that worked this week (25-29 Sep), with NPR article pages 503 for five consecutive days:** `opb.org`, `kpbs.org`, `stlpr.org`, `houstonpublicmedia.org`, `nprillinois.org`, `wusf.org`. The index at `text.npr.org` still fetches and remains the best headline list. **Standing caution, worth repeating because the search results make it easy to forget: a dozen stations running one NPR piece is ONE source.**
- **Senate and House committee press pages are primary for a vote's content (29 Sep).** `commerce.senate.gov/press/...` gave the Protect College Sports Act's provisions — the payment cap, the $22.5m retention and $5m Olympic funds, the 5% agent cap, the $600 reporting threshold — while congress.gov has 403'd for sixteen days and the bill text remains unreadable here. Pair the committee release with the roll call.

- **STANDING DISCIPLINE NOTE, added 30 Sep after the second correction on one story in three days. WHEN AN ITEM TURNS ON WHAT A COURT HAS OR HAS NOT DONE, READ THE DOCKET BEFORE ASSERTING WHAT IS NOT POSSIBLE.** On 26 September this brief said appropriated money not obligated by 30 September "generally lapses" and called it "impoundment by calendar"; on 28 September it repeated that "the appropriation runs out with no court able to move it". **Judge Cooper had suspended the statutory lapse on 21 SEPTEMBER — five days before the story first ran here.** The reasoning was sound and the fact was never checked, on a story whose entire subject was a court. This also reversed a conclusion in the 29 September pocket-rescission lead: a district court CAN suspend a statutory lapse, so the manoeuvre's effectiveness depends on nobody reaching a judge in time, not on arithmetic. The generalisable form, which is a cousin of the 28 September note about missing documents: **do not convert "I have not seen a remedy" into "there is no remedy".**
- **`supremecourt.gov/orders/...` has served no order data since 26 Sep (four attempts).** Two emergency-docket orders on 25 September and the third-country deportations order of 29 September all had to be reported second-hand. **Workarounds that produced the docket number, the vote alignment and the questions presented: a public radio station in the affected state (`stlpr.org` gave 26A388 and the quoted language), the winning agency's own press release (`dhs.gov` gave a case name and a vote), and `votebeat.org` for what an election order does in practice.** Add NBC and PBS for vote alignment on emergency applications.
- **THE FEDERAL REGISTER RETURNED ZERO DOCUMENTS OF ANY TYPE FOR WEDNESDAY 30 SEPTEMBER, and zero on public inspection**, after a full edition on the 29th. Both `documents.json` with no type filter and `public-inspection-documents.json` came back empty. This is not the weekend pattern and could not be explained. **Treat an unexplained zero on a business day as a SOURCE FAILURE and say so on the face of the issue — do not report it as "the government published nothing".** Re-check `public-inspection-documents.json?conditions[available_on]=YYYY-MM-DD` as the second opinion whenever `documents.json` returns 0 on a weekday.
- **A Conference Board consumer confidence release is worth a standing monthly check near the end of the month (30 Sep).** The September print produced a lead: index -6.7 to 81.9, Present Situation -7.9 to 109.3, Expectations -5.9 to 63.6. **Know the instrument: the Board itself says an Expectations reading below 80 is associated with recession within a year, so 63.6 is a signal and not just a low number — and the open-response detail (references to prices and to "oil and gas in particular") is what let this brief connect it to the fuel shock.** The Board's own release was not reached; CNBC, Axios and Al Jazeera carried the components.
- **A large story can be reachable through exactly one source, and the honest move is to run it and mark it.** The completed US withdrawal from Iraq on 29 September — including the removal of Patriot and C-RAM systems — was found only on Al Jazeera. No US or Iraqi official statement, no troop figure, no DoD confirmation of the air-defence removal. **Run it as a lead with Single-sourced and say in the confidence line exactly which claims rest on the one account.** Carried forward: a second source on the Patriot removal is still wanted.

- **THE 28 SEP RULE ABOUT ABSENCES WORKED THREE DAYS LATER, AND IT IS WORTH RECORDING THAT IT DID (1 Oct).** On Wednesday the `Federal Register` API returned **zero documents of any type**, and zero on public inspection, on a business day. The issue treated it as a **source failure** and said on its face that it could not tell whether the edition was real — rather than reporting that the government had published nothing. Today it returned **112** documents. **The generalisable pair, now tested in both directions: an absence is evidence about the channel, not about the world. Inferring substance from a missing document is what cost four issues on the Xi summit; declining to infer is what saved this one.**
- **A FULL FEDERAL REGISTER DAY AFTER A GAP IS THE HIGHEST-YIELD SWEEP THERE IS.** 1 October produced **three of seven leads** from four `full_text/text/` reads — the USDA REAP rewrite, the FCC copper-retirement rule and the FTC impersonation notice — plus a short. Running total: the whole-day sweep has produced an underreported lead in **five of the last eight runs**. **The tell remains a dull title with a near-term effective date**: "Unleashing American Energy and Economic Prosperity; Rural Energy for America Program" is a grant-eligibility rewrite, and "Reducing Barriers to Network Improvements" is the end of the copper telephone network.
- **Check whether a rule actually skipped notice and comment before adding it to the count.** The grants-exemption thread was at four instances; USDA REAP is a genuine fifth (and the only one to offer a comment period anyway). **The FCC copper rule looked like a sixth and is not** — its substance was adopted in a **26 March 2026 Report and Order** after proper notice and comment, and today's document only announced OMB paperwork approval. Saying so in the issue mattered: the procedural objection available against the others is not available against it. **Read the rule's own procedural history section rather than inferring from the effective date.**
- **`pinellas.gov/executive-orders/...` is the primary source for a Florida county emergency and its extensions (1 Oct).** Resolution **26-83** extended the dengue emergency to 8 October under authority delegated to the County Administrator, which is a different instrument from the Board resolution that declared it. **Local newsrooms carried the regional case count (179, from 143 a week earlier) and the West Nile sentinel-flock detection that the county page did not.** Use both. Hillsborough and Pasco do not publish equivalently and their decisions could not be found.
- **Sentinel chicken flocks are an arbovirus early-warning instrument and should be read as one.** Caged chickens are tested regularly for antibodies; a bird that seroconverts was bitten by an infected mosquito **at that location**. It is a direct reading of the mosquito population rather than the human one and leads case counts by weeks. **Know the vector difference too: dengue travels on *Aedes aegypti*, a container breeder with a short flight range; West Nile on *Culex*, which breed in standing water and range further — the same spraying does not do both jobs equally.**

- **`federalregister.gov/documents/full_text/text/...` CAN RETURN A NOT-FOUND PAGE FOR A DOCUMENT THAT EXISTS (2 Oct).** The Federal Scholarship Tax Credit final rule (**2026-20264**) appeared in the documents API and its full-text endpoint served a GovInfo "Page Not Found". That is the first failure of this endpoint on a document this brief wanted, after it had carried most of the week's underreported leads. **Fallbacks to try before giving up: the `/documents/<doc-number>` JSON for an abstract and a `body_html_url`; the GPO `govinfo.gov` PDF; or the agency's own newsroom.** A national school-voucher mechanism ran as a short with every number missing because none of those were tried in time — do better next time.
- **ON A VERY FULL DAY THE FAILURE MODE IS THIN VERIFICATION, NOT THIN SELECTION (2 Oct).** Eight leads ran and **four of eight short items** had to record that a document could not be read or a particular could not be got — the SEC quorum rule text, the scholarship rule, the SNAP mechanism, the Renee Good filing. **The discipline when the day is overloaded: cut the short tier rather than running items off headlines, or state the gap in the confidence line. Never let volume quietly lower the verification bar.**
- **A sourced private threat and a public cooling are not a contradiction to adjudicate — they are a sequence to date (2 Oct).** Reuters had the administration telling France and Germany to release diesel stocks **or face a US export ban**; CNBC the next day had Trump **cooling** on a ban as Hormuz flows recovered. Both can be true in order. **The rule: when two well-sourced accounts of an intention conflict, say which is older, say that leverage works better undeclared, and name the instrument that would settle it — here a Federal Register notice or a BIS export control, because a ban cannot be imposed by press conference.**
- **Watch the word "relieve" in a carrier deployment story.** Several accounts called the USS Theodore Roosevelt a **third carrier** near Iran; the Roosevelt is **expected to relieve** the USS George Washington, so a relief rotation and a net increase are different things and which it is depends on whether the Washington actually leaves. **The unambiguous signal in that deployment was the amphibious group: 2,000+ Marines on the Makin Island is the capability to put people ashore, which is a different message from air power.**
- **A belligerent's claim is not a count.** The IRGC Navy said it destroyed **ten commercial vessels** transiting Hormuz. One source, no names, no flags, no independent confirmation. **Report it as a claim with the claimant named, never as a figure, and keep looking for the physical series** — gCaptain's transit counts and ship-to-ship transfer capacity, both last read 28 September and still the only instruments that would adjudicate the "flows are recovering" assertion.

- **THE WORST SOURCING MISTAKE AVAILABLE HERE, AND IT WAS MADE AND CAUGHT ON 3 OCT: NEVER PUT THE EXPECTED VALUE IN THE REQUEST.** Treasury's September page was asked to find the table containing the header "10 Yr", return the value under it, *and* cross-check that 18 September should be 4.86. It came back with 4.86 confirmed and a full month running 4.55–5.09. **Those are the FIVE-YEAR column.** The request was answered by shifting two columns left to satisfy the number supplied in the question. Had it been published, the issue would have run a prominent correction retracting the 25 September figures as 15bp too high — and the retraction would have been false, a fourth wrong print of the same number in nineteen days. **A verification value supplied inside the request is not a verification, it is an instruction, and the extractor will obey it.** Verify against something internal to the data instead.
- **THE 21 SEP COLUMN RULE STANDS AND WAS VINDICATED AGAIN (3 Oct). Enumerate the headers in the same request and take every cell in POSITION order; never ask for a value "under" a header label.** The read that worked asked for all fifteen headers numbered and every cell in order, giving position 13 = 10 Yr. The second confirming test is **neighbour monotonicity** — yields rise with maturity, so 18 Sep reading 5 Yr=4.86, 7 Yr=4.93, 10 Yr=5.01, 20 Yr=5.38 is self-consistent and exposed 4.86 as the five-year. External confirmation came from market reporting putting Friday's ten-year at 5.281%, which matches position 13 (5.28) and not position 12 (7 Yr, 5.17). **Enumerate, then sanity-check the neighbours, every time.**
- **TREASURY DATE TRAP (3 Oct): `field_tdr_date_value_month` is YYYYMM and a WRONG YEAR RETURNS A PLAUSIBLE TABLE.** `…&field_tdr_date_value_month=202509` silently served **September 2025** — real dates, real yields, nothing to flag the error except the year in the date column. A mistyped year is indistinguishable from a correct read unless the dates are checked. Also: **the page can serve MORE THAN ONE TABLE** (a request on 3 Oct returned a fifteen-column layout ordered 20 YR, 30 YR, Extrapolation Factor, bill discounts, 1 Mo, 1.5 Mo, 2 Mo — long-term and bill tables concatenated), which is why position counting must be paired with reading the enumerated headers that came back, not with a memorised index.
- **Verified figure worth keeping: the October 2023 ten-year peak is 5.19%, on 17 AND 18 October 2023**, read with the enumerated-header method from Treasury's October 2023 table on 3 Oct. The ten-year crossed 5% in this cycle on **23 September 2026 (5.11)**; September's high was **5.29% on 30 September**. **"Highest since July 2007" STAYS WITHDRAWN** — the thirty-four months between November 2023 and September 2026 have not been walked, so the superlative is still unavailable. The narrower true form is "above the October 2023 peak of 5.19%, verified directly".
- **THE PRESDOCU QUERY IS NOT OPTIONAL AND SKIPPING IT COST FIVE DOCUMENTS ON 2 OCT.** The 2 October run read that day's RULE/PRORULE output (two leads came from it) and did not run the presidential-documents query. Friday carried **five** PRESDOCU items — EO 14434 (renaming AI), EO 14433 (pests), EO 14432 (America.gov), the FY2027 refugee determination, and the Syria national-emergency continuation — which became three leads and a short a day late. **This exact trap is recorded in this file dated 14 September. Run all three queries (RULE+PRORULE, PRESDOCU, NOTICE) every run; it is three calls.**
- **`federalregister.gov/documents/full_text/text/YYYY/MM/DD/DOC-NUM.txt` worked on five of five documents on 3 Oct**, including all three executive orders and the SEC quorum rule that could not be reached the day before. So **2 October's 404 on the scholarship tax credit was document-specific, not an endpoint failure** — retry a failed document the next day rather than concluding the endpoint is down.
- **`gasprices.aaa.com` and `eia.gov/dnav/pet/pet_pri_gnd_dcus_nus_w.htm` both fetched cleanly on a Saturday (3 Oct)**, which is the pairing the 22 September instrument rule requires: AAA daily for the level, EIA weekly for the trend. On 3 Oct they agreed on direction for the first time since the spike began, which is what licensed calling the turn.
- **`floridahealth.gov/diseases-and-conditions/mosquito-borne-diseases/surveillance.html` does NOT carry the data** (3 Oct) — it only links onward to `statistics-data/population-surveillance/arbovirus-surveillance/`, which is the page that establishes whether a week has published. The weekly report PDF remains unparseable, a tenth consecutive issue. **County dengue counts are reachable through local reporting of FDOH figures: WUSF's health desk, WFLA and Bay News 9 all carried them on 1–2 October.**
- **`nhc.noaa.gov/text/MIATWOAT.shtml` fetched cleanly (3 Oct)** and gave the 2am outlook: no formation expected over seven days. Keep the standing check through November.
- **`fox13news.com/tag/us/fl/pasco` remains the best Tampa Bay local sweep** (3 Oct, confirmed again) and carried the dengue emergency declarations. `wfla.com` returned usable search results this run despite the 403 recorded on 13 September — worth one attempt rather than assuming it is blocked.
- **Wikipedia's current-events portal was populated for BOTH 2 and 3 October when read on the morning of the 3rd** — a better showing than 17 September, when it was empty on the day. It supplied the G7 reserve release, the French lycée protests and the Latvian election. Still tertiary; still the best list of what happened that you have not seen.
- **`gcaptain.com` carried the Hormuz vessel count, the carrier deployment and the Saudi Bab el-Mandeb plan before any reachable general wire (3 Oct)** — the third run in which it was the only source for a chokepoint measurement. It still does not carry war-risk premiums.
- **`openai.com` 403s for a fourth recorded occasion (3 Oct), and anthropic.com's release pages were likewise unreachable**, so both the GPT-Synopsys agreement and the two model launches are second-hand. **AIwire (hpcwire.com/aiwire), The Decoder and Engineering.com all fetched** and together gave the full terms of the Synopsys agreement.
- **Al Jazeera live blogs give headlines but frequently not the detail (3 Oct):** the Iran live blog's Hormuz entry yielded "two oil tankers struck by unidentified projectiles" and nothing else — no names, flags, damage or casualties. The index page remains the fast morning sweep; do not expect a live blog to stand up a figure.

- **THE RULE BROKEN YESTERDAY AND REPEATED TODAY IN A NEW PLACE: FIGURES FROM SEPARATE REPORTS DESCRIBE SEPARATE MOMENTS AND CANNOT BE ADDED.** The 3 Oct Florida headline said "Tampa Bay counts 239 dengue cases" — a sum of Hillsborough 223, Pinellas 13 and Pasco 3, three numbers published on different days by different outlets. Florida's statewide 2026 total is **252**, of which Hillsborough is **223**; a separately reported **Tampa Bay regional figure of 179 confirmed cases** also circulates and does not reconcile with the sum. Corrected in a box at the top of the 4 Oct Florida story. **This file already carried the rule twice for transit counts (daily vs weekly Hormuz figures, 21 Sep) and it was not applied to case counts. It is now recorded against both.**
- **A SUNDAY RUN HAS NO RULE-MAKING CHANNEL AT ALL, AND THAT IS WORTH SAYING IN THE ISSUE.** The *Federal Register* does not publish Saturdays or Sundays and its public-inspection list is offline until Monday 8:45am Eastern, so a Sunday issue has no view of Monday's documents either. Treasury publishes no weekend yield curve. On 4 Oct this removed the source that had produced four of the previous day's eight leads. **What carried the issue instead: Wikipedia's current-events portal, which supplied the Iraqi throughput figure that contradicted Iran's closure claim — the single most useful fact in the top story — and gCaptain, which again produced three items no general wire carried.**
- **`npr.org` article pages returned HTTP 503 on both attempts (4 Oct)**, a different failure from the 60-second timeouts recorded 15 and 17 September. The `text.npr.org` index fetched cleanly as always. Two leads had to be built from other sources, and one of them — the Supreme Court term — could only know NPR's framing from its headline, which was disclosed in the divergence box. **Budget for this: text.npr.org is a headline list and reaching the story behind a headline fails more often than it works.**
- **`iranwire.com` returns 403 (4 Oct)** and it was the one source that appeared to enumerate Iran's seven Hormuz conditions. **`thenationalnews.com` fetches cleanly and was the best source on the Iranian statements**, giving Ghalibaf's exact words, the Qatari mediation channel and the Araghchi seven-day offer. `cyprus-mail.com` carries Reuters copy and fetches.
- **WHEN NO SOURCE PUBLISHES A LIST, SAY THE LIST IS YOURS.** No reachable account gave all seven Iranian conditions; two partial accounts together named seven distinct demands. The 4 Oct issue printed the enumeration with the words "this enumeration is assembled by this brief, not quoted from a document", and noted that two items may be one condition counted twice. **That is the form to reuse: give the reader the best available reconstruction, label the construction, and name the uncertainty inside it.**
- **`scotusblog.com` fetches cleanly and is the way to get an argument session (4 Oct)**, but its session post names only the lead case; the full calendar lives in a **supremecourt.gov PDF that cannot be parsed** here. `constitutioncenter.org` and `ballotpedia.org` both fetch and together give the granted docket, though without argument dates. **So: first case and sitting dates are obtainable; a full dated calendar is not.**
- **`hcfl.gov/newsroom` fetches cleanly** and is the primary source for a Hillsborough County emergency order — better than local reporting of it. Worth trying before the TV stations for anything the county itself does. `winknews.com` carried the Florida statewide dengue total that Tampa-market outlets did not.
- **`gcaptain.com` is now the single most productive off-watchlist source** and produced four items on 4 Oct alone (the Odesa strike, port congestion, Alaska LNG costs, the Cuba interdiction) plus the Hormuz vessel count. Its weakness is that front-page headlines often carry no detail: the Odesa and congestion items had to run as thin shorts because no vessel name or methodology was available.
- **`iranwire`, `apnews`, `openai.com`, `congress.gov`, `cnn.com` and `japantimes.co.jp` are now all reliably blocked.** Stop spending attempts on them; go to the primary document, a member station, or a syndicating outlet first.

- **THE COLUMN TRAP RECURRED A THIRD WAY ON 5 OCT AND THE FILE ITSELF CAUGHT IT.** The Treasury read enumerated **twenty-four** column headers and then returned **twenty-six** positions per row, with values shifted **twelve places** - it named position 13 as the ten-year while reporting position 13 as 4.06. It was recognised as misaligned because **the values matched figures already recorded in this file on 3 October**: 1 Mo 4.06 and 10 Yr 5.24 for 1 October, 10 Yr 5.28 for 2 October. **So the data was right and the labelling was wrong, and the check that caught it was comparison against the archive, not anything in the request.** Three distinct failure modes on this one table in three weeks (label-shift, planted-expected-value, header/cell count mismatch), and twice in three days the thing that saved it was an internal consistency check. **Always carry two or three known values from the file into the comparison after the read - never into the prompt.**
- **TREASURY PUBLISHES NO WEEKEND CURVE AND MONDAY'S POSTS IN THE EVENING.** On a Monday morning run the table still ends at the previous Friday. On 5 October that meant no yields story, and none was forced - worth stating in the issue rather than reaching for a stale level.
- **RUN ALL THREE FEDERAL REGISTER QUERIES AND READ THE WHOLE LIST: on 5 Oct that produced three of eight leads and five of eight shorts from 26 rules and 80 notices.** Two leads came from the same tell recorded on 16 September and now proven repeatedly: **REPETITION IS THE POLICY.** Six SEC notices on professional-credential designations are one proposal to redefine the accredited investor; seven FAA proposed rules on Part 450 are one rewrite of launch licensing. **Neither exists anywhere as a single document with a single name, and no wire carried either.** The largest item of the day - the FinCEN A7 special measure, with $179.1bn of stablecoin volume in its findings - had no secondary coverage at all.
- **`federalregister.gov/documents/full_text/text/...` worked on ELEVEN of eleven attempts on 5 October**, including a presidential determination, a FinCEN special measure, two SEC proposals, an FAA NPRM, an NRC final rule, HHS, HUD, Treasury, CPSC and Census. The endpoint is now the single most reliable source available to this brief. **Never write from an abstract when it exists.**
- **`insurancejournal.com` FETCHED for the first time on 5 October**, having refused the connection outright on 14 September. Worth re-trying sources recorded as blocked - the list goes stale in both directions. Likewise **`edition.cnn.com` resolved through search** where the bare `cnn.com` has returned 451 since 13 September; use the `edition.` host.
- **`stripes.com` fetches cleanly and is the best source for anything involving US forces abroad (5 Oct)** - it had the Okinawa arrest with the suspect's unit, base, the victim's name and the police timeline before the general wires carried the detail. Standing check for the Futenma and allied-basing threads.
- **WHEN A DOCUMENT IS ONLY A PROCEDURAL NOTICE, SAY SO AND NAME THE DOCUMENT YOU DID NOT READ.** The CPSC item on 5 October looked like a lithium-ion battery rule and was a **hearing notice** for a 24 June NPRM; the Census item was an **extension notice** for a 10 September proposal. Both ran with the underlying document named as unread and owed. **The index title does not tell you which kind of document it is - the full text does.**

- **A NEW SOURCING LIMIT, AND IT IS A REAL ONE: SOME FEDERAL REGISTER DOCUMENTS ARE TOO LARGE TO READ.** On 6 October the SEC crypto custody proposal ran to about **1,638,000 characters**, the Transparency in Coverage rule **790,000**, and the performance-fee proposal **517,000** - and the full-text retrieval returns the **first 100,000** and says so. For the first time this brief reported on major rules from their OPENING SECTIONS, with the issue saying plainly that figures beyond those quoted were not established. **This is a different failure from a blocked page or an unparseable PDF: the document is readable and will not fit. THE FIX IS TO REQUEST SUCCESSIVE OFFSETS (`offset: 100000`, `200000`, ...), which the retrieval note itself offers.** Budget two or three extra calls for any rule with a cost-benefit analysis, and never assume the operative provisions are in the first 100,000 characters - preambles are long and the regulatory text is at the end.
- **READING TWO CONSECUTIVE DAYS OF THE SAME SOURCE PRODUCED THE 6 OCTOBER LEAD, AND NOTHING CLEVERER THAN THAT WAS NEEDED.** 5 Oct: FinCEN proposes a special measure premised on reaching crypto addresses. 6 Oct: FinCEN withdraws the two general rules that would have reached them. **Three documents in one week from one bureau pointing in opposite directions, and no wire reachable here carried any of them. The sequence is invisible to anyone reading one day's output.** Treat consecutive-day comparison as a standing method, not a lucky catch.
- **THE TREASURY COLUMN TRAP RECURRED FOR A FOURTH TIME (6 Oct) AND THE ARCHIVE CAUGHT IT AGAIN.** The read returned **twenty-four enumerated headers and twenty-six cells per row**, with the series offset by **eleven** places. Resolved by reconciling the 1 and 2 October values in the response against what this file recorded on 3 October, which fixed the offset and identified the ten-year at reported-position 24. **The rule is now proven three mornings running: enumerate the headers in the request, take the cells in position order, then reconcile against values already in the file - and NEVER put the expected value in the prompt.** Also confirmed: **Treasury posts the prior business day's curve, so a Tuesday morning run gets Monday.**
- **`everycrsreport.com` earned its place again (6 Oct)** as the statutory-mechanics workaround while **congress.gov has returned 403 for twenty-three consecutive days**. Report R49419 gave the full posture of *Suncor v. Boulder*. For the PBM story the equivalents were **law-firm client alerts (Mintz, Buchanan) plus KFF and AJMC**, which are the only reachable route to what a statute enacted inside a spending bill actually does. **When congress.gov is blocked, law-firm alerts are the substitute for bill text - cite them as secondary and say the text was not read.**
- **SUPREME COURT ARGUMENTS ARE NOT REACHABLE THE NEXT MORNING.** On 6 October no transcript, audio or argument readout for the previous day's *Suncor* argument could be found, so the term's first case ran as a SHORT saying exactly that rather than as a lead. **Pre-argument primers (Legal Planet, Harvard EELP, CRS) are reachable and give posture and questions presented; what the justices said is not. Plan the issue accordingly - an argument story written the morning after will be about the case, not the hearing.**
- **FOUR SEC PROPOSALS IN TWO DAYS WERE FOUND ONLY BY READING THE WHOLE LIST BOTH DAYS.** Accredited-investor credentials and interval funds on 5 Oct; performance-based compensation and crypto custody on 6 Oct. **None was carried by any wire. The repetition tell works across days as well as within one: a bureau filing in the same direction on consecutive mornings is running a programme, and the programme has no single document or name.**

- **A THIN RULES LIST IS NOT A THIN DAY: READ THE NOTICES.** On 7 October the *Federal Register* carried only **11** rules and proposed rules, the thinnest in two weeks, and **62** notices - and the notices produced **two leads and four shorts**. The IRS Trump Account estimate (148.5M responses, 71.2M burden hours) and the Copyright Office streaming-fraud inquiry were both buried among routine information collections, and neither was carried by any wire reachable here. **When the rules list is short, the temptation is to conclude the day is quiet; the notices list is where it was.**
- **THE TREASURY TABLE READ CORRECTLY ON 7 OCTOBER - fifteen headers, fifteen cells, properly aligned, values matching the archive - after FOUR consecutive mornings of header/cell mismatch.** So the fault is **INTERMITTENT, NOT PERMANENT**, and the consequence is counter-intuitive: **a clean-looking read is no longer evidence of alignment, so the reconciliation step stays mandatory every single time.** The rule is unchanged: enumerate the headers in the request, take the cells in position order, reconcile against values already in this file, and never put the expected value in the prompt.
